### Texas State Cemetery — The Arlington of Texas
**ID**: `texas-state-cemetery` | **Type**: place | **Years**: 1851–present | **Moments**: 8

> Explore the 22 acres where the complicated soul of Texas is interred. From Republic-era heroes and Confederate generals to civil rights icons and modern governors, these hallowed grounds serve as a physical map of the state's evolution from a wild frontier to a global power.

**50** · **Stephen F. Austin's Reburial** (1851)
*The Father of Texas returns to the capital*
The cemetery was established in 1851 specifically to provide a final resting place for Stephen F. Austin. He had died penniless in a small cabin in 1836, and his body was moved here from a remote plantation. His massive monument at the center of the grounds established the site as the state's primary secular shrine.
 | Entities: texas-state-cemetery, stephen-f-austin

**26** · **First Southern Black Congresswoman Buried Among Texas Elite** (1996)
*A voice for the Constitution in the heart of Texas*
Barbara Jordan, the first Southern Black woman in Congress, is buried here, making her one of the most prominent African American figures in the cemetery. Her grave stands in a section that once only honored the state's white elite, a powerful symbol of the progress of civil rights in Texas.
 | Entities: texas-state-cemetery, barbara-jordan

**17** · **Governor Bush Restores the Neglected State Cemetery** (1994)
*Transforming a neglected yard into a national monument*
By the early 1990s, the cemetery had fallen into serious disrepair, with toppled stones and overgrown weeds. Governor George W. Bush championed a $4.8 million restoration that added a state-of-the-art visitors center and extensive new landscaping. The project transformed the site into an active memorial and a major tourist attraction.
 | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

**18** · **Elisabet Ney Memorializes General Albert Sidney Johnston** (1905)
*A masterful marble tribute to a fallen Confederate commander*
The grave of Albert Sidney Johnston is marked by a stunning recumbent statue by renowned sculptor Elisabet Ney. Johnston, the highest-ranking officer killed in the Civil War, was originally buried in New Orleans before being reinterred here in 1867. The monument is considered one of the finest pieces of funerary art in the United States.
Kind: presence | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

**18** · **Governor James Hogg Is Interred Under a Pecan Tree** (1906)
*A "People's Governor" whose grave produces food for the public*
James Stephen Hogg, the first native-born Governor of Texas, requested that no monument be built for him, but instead that a pecan tree be planted at his head so the nuts could be distributed to the people of Texas. His grave became a symbol of populist spirit, leading to the pecan being named the official state tree of Texas in 1919.
Kind: milestone | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

**17** · **Governor John Connally Is Buried Near Political Allies** (1993)
*The final resting place of a titan of 20th-century Texas politics*
John Connally, who served as Texas Governor and U.S. Treasury Secretary, was buried here following his death in 1993. Most famous nationally for being wounded during the JFK assassination in Dallas, Connally's grave in the Republic Hill section places him among the giants of Texas history he spent his life emulating.
Kind: milestone | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

**17** · **Author James Michener Joins the Ranks of Texas Legends** (1997)
*A New Yorker who researched his way into the heart of the state*
Pulitzer Prize-winning author James Michener was granted a rare plot in the State Cemetery despite not being a native Texan, in recognition of his monumental novel 'Texas.' Michener spent years in Austin researching the state's history, and his burial here reflects the unique way Texas adopts those who tell its story with sufficient scale.
Kind: milestone | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

**18** · **Governor Ann Richards Is Laid to Rest with a Final Wit** (2006)
*The "Silver Fox" takes her place in the hallowed grounds*
The burial of Ann Richards in 2006 drew thousands to the cemetery to honor the state's second female governor. Her grave, located in a prominent section of the grounds, serves as a pilgrimage site for those who admired her sharp tongue and progressive leadership. Her presence here ensures the cemetery remains a living monument to the diversity of Texas political thought.
Kind: milestone | Entities: texas-state-cemetery

### Paris Under Fire
**ID**: `paris-under-fire` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1871–2019 | **Moments**: 6

> The commune that burned the Tuileries, the affair that split a nation, the treaty that seeded another war, a liberation march under sniper fire, students who nearly toppled a government, and a cathedral that burned on live television.

**50** · **The Paris Commune Burns the Tuileries Palace During Bloody Week** (1871)
*A revolutionary government holds Paris for seventy-two days before the army massacres 20,000*
In March 1871, after France's defeat in the Franco-Prussian War, Parisian workers and National Guard members seized control of the city and established the Paris Commune — a radical democratic government that lasted 72 days. When the French Army besieged and retook Paris during "Bloody Week" (May 21-28), the Communards set fire to the Tuileries Palace, the Hôtel de Ville, and other buildings. The army's reprisals were savage: an estimated 20,000 Communards were killed, many summarily executed. The Tuileries Palace was never rebuilt. Karl Marx called the Commune the first dictatorship of the proletariat.
Kind: event

**25** · **Captain Dreyfus Is Publicly Stripped of His Rank in a Case That Tears France Apart** (1895)
*A Jewish officer is framed for treason and the resulting scandal redefines French politics for a decade*
On January 5, 1895, Captain Alfred Dreyfus was publicly degraded in the courtyard of the École Militaire before 5,000 spectators. His sword was broken, his insignia torn off, and he was paraded before troops while the crowd screamed anti-Semitic slurs. Dreyfus had been convicted of passing military secrets to Germany based on forged evidence. When evidence emerged that the real traitor was Major Ferdinand Walsin Esterhazy, the Army covered it up. Émile Zola's famous front-page letter "J'accuse" in 1898 blew the case open, splitting France into Dreyfusards and anti-Dreyfusards. Dreyfus was eventually exonerated in 1906.
Kind: event

**25** · **The Allies Force Germany to Sign the Treaty That Ends One War and Seeds Another** (1919)
*In the Hall of Mirrors where Bismarck crowned a German emperor, Germany is humiliated*
On June 28, 1919, in the Hall of Mirrors at the Palace of Versailles — the same room where the German Empire had been proclaimed in 1871 — German delegates signed the Treaty of Versailles ending World War I. Germany was forced to accept sole responsibility for the war, pay massive reparations, cede territory, and disarm. The German delegation called it a "Diktat." The treaty's humiliating terms fueled German resentment for two decades. Economist John Maynard Keynes warned it would lead to another war. Adolf Hitler built his political career on promising to overturn it.
Kind: event

**38** · **De Gaulle Walks Down the Champs-Élysées as Snipers Fire from Rooftops** (1944)
*The leader of Free France reclaims Paris while bullets fly over his head*
On August 26, 1944, the day after Paris was liberated from German occupation, Charles de Gaulle led a triumphal march down the Champs-Élysées from the Arc de Triomphe to Notre-Dame Cathedral. As he walked through massive jubilant crowds, snipers — possibly German holdouts, possibly Vichy militia — opened fire from rooftops near the Hôtel de Ville and inside Notre-Dame itself. De Gaulle barely flinched. The march cemented his image as the embodiment of French resistance and sovereignty, and his political dominance of postwar France.
Kind: event | Entities: charles-de-gaulle

**38** · **Students Build Barricades in the Latin Quarter and Nearly Topple the Government** (1968)
*What begins as a university protest becomes the largest general strike in French history*
In May 1968, student protests at the University of Paris escalated into a national crisis. On the night of May 10-11 — the "Night of the Barricades" — students tore up cobblestones and overturned cars in the Latin Quarter, fighting running battles with riot police. When images of police brutality shocked the nation, workers joined in solidarity. By May 22, ten million workers were on strike — the largest general strike in French history. President de Gaulle secretly fled to a French military base in Germany to ensure the army's loyalty. The government survived, but French society was permanently transformed.
Kind: event | Entities: charles-de-gaulle

**25** · **Notre-Dame Cathedral Burns for Fifteen Hours as the World Watches** (2019)
*The medieval spire collapses into the nave of an 850-year-old cathedral on live television*
On the evening of April 15, 2019, a fire broke out in the attic of Notre-Dame Cathedral, likely caused by an electrical short-circuit or a discarded cigarette during renovation work. The medieval oak roof structure — 13,000 trees felled in the 12th and 13th centuries — burned for over fifteen hours. The iconic 96-meter spire, added by Viollet-le-Duc in the 19th century, collapsed into the nave on live television as crowds gathered along the Seine in tears. Firefighters saved the main structure, the rose windows, and the Crown of Thorns relic. Within days, over €1 billion in reconstruction pledges poured in from around the world.
Kind: event

### The Pueblo Revolt
**ID**: `pueblo-revolt` | **Type**: incident | **Years**: 1680 | **Moments**: 1

> In 1680 — nearly a century before the American Revolution — Pueblo peoples united across language barriers and hundreds of miles to drive the Spanish Empire entirely out of New Mexico. It remains the most successful Native uprising in North American history.

**47** · **Pueblo Warriors Seize the Palace of the Governors** (1680)
*Coordinated uprising forces Spanish retreat from Santa Fe for 12 years*
On August 10, 1680, coordinated by the Tewa leader Pope, thousands of Pueblo warriors besieged Santa Fe. Governor Antonio de Otermin retreated to the Palace of the Governors with 1,000 settlers. After the water supply was cut, the Spanish fled south. The Pueblo people held the palace and all of New Mexico for 12 years until the Spanish returned in 1692.

### Palace of the Governors
**ID**: `palace-of-governors` | **Type**: place | **Years**: 1610–present | **Moments**: 1

> Built in 1610, the Palace of the Governors is the oldest continuously occupied public building in the United States. It has seen Spanish, Pueblo, Mexican, and American flags fly overhead across four centuries of dramatic history.

**46** · **Four Flags Fly Over America's Oldest Public Building** (1610)
*Spain, Pueblo rebels, Mexico, the Confederacy, and America have all governed from this one adobe*
Built in 1610 as the seat of Spanish colonial government, this adobe structure has served under the flags of Spain, the Pueblo people (1680-1692), Mexico (1821-1846), the Confederacy (briefly in 1862), and the United States. Native artisans have sold jewelry and pottery under its portal since the 1880s — a tradition protected by city ordinance today.

### The Confederate West
**ID**: `confederate-mesilla` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1862 | **Moments**: 1

> In 1862, the Confederate flag flew over Mesilla when it briefly served as the capital of the Confederate Territory of Arizona. The Confederacy's westernmost ambitions reached deep into the New Mexico desert before being turned back at Glorieta Pass.

**39** · **Confederates Declare Mesilla the Capital of Arizona Territory** (1862)
*Where the Confederate flag flew over the Rio Grande in 1862*
In 1861, Confederate sympathizers in the Mesilla Valley declared their allegiance to the South. When Confederate Colonel John Baylor arrived with his Texas Mounted Rifles, Mesilla became the capital of the newly declared Confederate Territory of Arizona. The rebel flag flew over this plaza until Union forces reclaimed the territory after the Battle of Glorieta Pass in March 1862 — sometimes called the "Gettysburg of the West."

*A camera captures the shots that shook the city*
On October 27, 2006, Indymedia reporter Brad Will is filming a barricade. As he records plainclothes paramilitaries firing at protesters, two bullets hit him in the chest. His camera continues to run as he falls, capturing the sounds of the struggle to save him.

**17** · **Brad Will Pronounced Dead at the Red Cross Clinic** (2006)

**34** · **Engineers Dig a New Riverbed to Settle the Border Dispute** (1963)
*Where the border was physically relocated with concrete*
In 1963, Presidents Kennedy and López Mateos agreed to end the dispute. Engineers dug a new, concrete-lined riverbed to ensure the border would never move again. Thousands of homes were demolished to make way for the new channel.

**16** · **Rio Grande Flood Shifts the Border, Creating a Century of Dispute** (1864)
*The "no man's land" that moved south*
This location marks the original path of the Rio Grande before the flood of 1864. For a century, this tract of land was a source of constant tension between El Paso and Juárez, as residents lived on land that two nations claimed simultaneously.

### The Barton Springs Development Fights
**ID**: `barton-springs-sos` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1990 | **Moments**: 2

> In a single night, 800 citizens spoke at a city council meeting for 15 straight hours to save a swimming hole from corporate development. This grassroots explosion birthed the Save Our Springs (SOS) Alliance.

**33** · **Environmentalists Fight Development to Save the Barton Springs Salamander** (1990)
*Natural springs become the catalyst for Austin's strict environmental laws*
The natural Barton Springs Pool became the legal centerpiece of a fight to stop a massive corporate development called Barton Creek PUD. Conservationists leveraged the endangered status of the Barton Springs Salamander to push for new water-quality regulations. This conflict forever defined Austin as a city that would engage in intense political warfare to protect its natural resources.
Kind: presence | Entities: barton-springs-salamander, save-our-springs-alliance

**7** · **Eight Hundred Citizens Flood an All-Night Public Hearing at City Hall** (1990)
*Residents speak for 15 hours straight to successfully reject a development plan*
On June 7, 1990, over 800 citizens flooded a City Council meeting in what became known as "The All-Night Public Hearing." Residents spoke against the Barton Creek development for 15 straight hours until the sun rose. This grassroots explosion birthed the Save Our Springs (SOS) Alliance and successfully pressured the city to reject the massive construction project.

### The 1928 Master Plan
**ID**: `austin-1928-plan` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1928 | **Moments**: 3

> A single city-commissioned document legally forced an entire population to migrate to one side of the highway, intentionally creating the 'East Side' and destroying flourishing Black communities in the West. This bureaucratic erasure permanently etched segregation into Austin's asphalt.

**24** · **Austin City Council Adopts 1928 Master Plan Creating a 'Negro District'** (1928)
*A document codifies segregation and reshapes the city's geography*
The Austin City Council formally adopted the 1928 Master Plan, which recommended the creation of a 'Negro District.' By concentrating all schools and services for Black residents in East Austin, the city effectively enforced racial segregation without passing illegal zoning laws. This document permanently reshaped the city's geography and displaced thriving Black communities in the West.
Kind: event | Entities: austin-1928-plan

**12** · **City Disconnects Services to Force Migration East of East Avenue** (1928)
*Bureaucratic pressure forces families to relocate to the segregated district*
East Avenue (now I-35) became the hard border for the new district. Families who refused to move East found their water and electricity disconnected by the city. This forced migration was designed to empty thriving communities like Wheatville and Clarksville to make way for white development.
Kind: event | Entities: austin-1928-plan

**11** · **City Closes Wheatville School to Displace Black Residents** (1928)
*The removal of community anchors enforces the segregated Master Plan*
To force the migration, the city closed the Wheatville School, the primary educational center for Black families in West Austin. With no local school and no municipal services, the residents were left with no choice but to abandon their property and move to the designated district.
Kind: event | Entities: austin-1928-plan

### The Assassination of Luis Donaldo Colosio
**ID**: `colosio-assassination` | **Type**: incident | **Years**: 1994 | **Moments**: 1

> The man destined to be Mexico's next president is shot at point-blank range during a campaign rally while a specific song, "La Culebra," blares from the speakers. The murder is captured from multiple angles, yet most Mexicans believe the "lone gunman" was a decoy for a state-sponsored hit.

**20** · **The Lomas Taurinas Rally** (1994)
*The shots heard across the Republic*
On March 23, 1994, Colosio is walking through a densely packed crowd in a poor Tijuana neighborhood. Mario Aburto Martínez presses a .38 Taurus against the candidate's temple and fires. A second shot hits him in the abdomen. The chaotic footage of the crowd surging as the music continues to play remains one of the most traumatizing images in Mexican political history.

### The 1860 Dallas Fire Conspiracy
**ID**: `dallas-fire-1860` | **Type**: incident | **Years**: 1860 | **Moments**: 2

> A devastating fire that leveled downtown Dallas was blamed on an "Abolitionist Plot," leading to a state-wide panic and the lynching of three Black men on the banks of the Trinity River.

**16** · **Fire Destroys Dallas Business District, Igniting Slave Panic** (1860)
*Where 110-degree heat turned a spark into an inferno*
On July 8, 1860, a fire destroyed most of the Dallas business district. Hysteria gripped the town as leaders claimed it was a slave uprising. This "Texas Troubles" panic led to vigilante groups patrolling the state looking for abolitionist spies.

**8** · **Mob Lynches Three Enslaved Men as Scapegoats** (1860)
*The execution of Patrick, Samuel, and Cato*
Without evidence, three enslaved men were seized and taken to the riverbank. They were lynched by a mob as the town sought scapegoats for the disaster. The event pushed Texas further toward secession and the Civil War.

## ARTS & CULTURE

### Literary Titans
**ID**: `literary-titans` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1599–1973 | **Moments**: 7

> Shakespeare building the Globe, Dostoevsky surviving a mock execution, Tolstoy dying in a train station, García Márquez pawning his car to finish a masterpiece, Kafka writing through the night, and Tagore winning Asia's first Nobel — the writers who defined their civilizations.

**84** · **Shakespeare Opens the Globe Theatre on the South Bank of the Thames** (1599)
*A glove-maker's son from Stratford builds the most famous playhouse in history*
In 1599, William Shakespeare and his company dismantled their old theatre north of the Thames (after a lease dispute), carried the timbers across the river, and rebuilt them as the Globe Theatre in Southwark. Over the next 14 years, Shakespeare premiered his greatest works: Hamlet, Othello, King Lear, Macbeth, and The Tempest. In 1613, a cannon set the thatched roof ablaze during Henry VIII. Shakespeare retired to Stratford-upon-Avon and died in 1616.
Kind: event | Entities: william-shakespeare

**39** · **Dostoevsky Faces a Firing Squad — Then Is Told It Was a Hoax** (1849)
*A mock execution transforms a young writer into literature's greatest explorer of human suffering*
On December 22, 1849, Fyodor Dostoevsky and 20 other political prisoners were led to Semyonov Square in St. Petersburg, bound to stakes, and blindfolded before a firing squad. The soldiers raised their rifles. At the last moment, a messenger arrived with a "reprieve" — the entire execution had been staged as psychological torture. One prisoner went permanently insane on the spot. Dostoevsky was sentenced to four years of hard labor in Siberia. The experience shaped Crime and Punishment, The Brothers Karamazov, and The Idiot.
Kind: event | Entities: fyodor-dostoevsky

**39** · **Tolstoy Abandons His Estate at 82 and Dies in a Train Station** (1910)
*The author of War and Peace walks out on his family and fortune — and dies ten days later*
On October 28, 1910, 82-year-old Leo Tolstoy left his estate at Yasnaya Polyana in the middle of the night. He had spent decades torn between his aristocratic life and his conviction that private property was immoral. After ten days of confused travel, he developed pneumonia and was carried off at Astapovo station, where he died on November 7 in the stationmaster's house. His death made worldwide news. He was buried at Yasnaya Polyana with no religious ceremony, as he wished.
Kind: event | Entities: leo-tolstoy

**37** · **García Márquez Writes One Hundred Years of Solitude in 18 Months of Self-Imposed Poverty** (1967)
*A journalist pawns his car and heater to finish a novel that will sell 50 million copies*
In 1965, while driving from Mexico City to Acapulco, Gabriel García Márquez was struck by a sudden vision of the novel he had been trying to write for 15 years. He turned the car around and told his wife Mercedes he needed 18 months. She pawned their car, their heater, and a food mixer, and kept the household running on credit while he wrote every day. The result — One Hundred Years of Solitude — sold out its first print run in a week and has since sold over 50 million copies. It won him the Nobel Prize in 1982.
Kind: event | Entities: gabriel-garcia-marquez

**35** · **Pablo Neruda Dies Twelve Days After Pinochet's Coup Under Disputed Circumstances** (1973)
*Chile's greatest poet lies dying while soldiers burn books in the streets*
On September 23, 1973, twelve days after Pinochet's military coup, Pablo Neruda died at a Santiago clinic. The official cause was cancer, but in 2023, forensic experts concluded he did not die of cancer and found a toxic bacterium in his remains. Neruda had been Chile's most beloved poet, a senator, a communist, and the 1971 Nobel laureate. His houses were ransacked by the military. At his funeral, mourners chanted his poetry through the streets — one of the first public acts of resistance against the dictatorship.
Kind: event | Entities: pablo-neruda

**39** · **Kafka Writes The Trial While Working Full-Time at an Insurance Company in Prague** (1914)
*A man who burned 90% of his work becomes the most influential novelist of the 20th century*
In 1914, Franz Kafka began writing The Trial — about a man arrested for an unspecified crime — while working as a senior clerk at the Workers' Accident Insurance Institute in Prague. He wrote at night, often from 11 PM to 6 AM, then went to work. He never finished and asked his friend Max Brod to burn all his manuscripts. Brod refused. The Trial, The Castle, and Amerika were published posthumously and became foundational texts of modern literature. The word "Kafkaesque" entered every major language.
Kind: event | Entities: franz-kafka

**39** · **Rabindranath Tagore Wins the Nobel Prize — the First Non-European Laureate** (1913)
*A Bengali poet who wrote the national anthems of two countries changes how the West sees Asia*
In 1913, Rabindranath Tagore won the Nobel Prize in Literature for Gitanjali, becoming the first non-European laureate. Tagore was a poet, novelist, playwright, painter, and composer who wrote roughly 2,230 songs, including the national anthems of both India and Bangladesh. He founded Visva-Bharati University at Santiniketan as an alternative to colonial education, teaching classes outdoors under trees. He renounced his British knighthood after the 1919 Amritsar massacre.
Kind: milestone | Entities: rabindranath-tagore

### London's Great Stages
**ID**: `london-great-stages` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1599–1969 | **Moments**: 5

> Newton's laws of the universe, the key to Egyptian hieroglyphics, a glass cathedral of human achievement, and the Beatles' final performance — the moments when London was the stage for ideas that changed everything.

**84** · **Shakespeare Opens the Globe Theatre on the South Bank of the Thames** (1599)
*A glove-maker's son from Stratford builds the most famous playhouse in history*
In 1599, William Shakespeare and his company dismantled their old theatre north of the Thames (after a lease dispute), carried the timbers across the river, and rebuilt them as the Globe Theatre in Southwark. Over the next 14 years, Shakespeare premiered his greatest works: Hamlet, Othello, King Lear, Macbeth, and The Tempest. In 1613, a cannon set the thatched roof ablaze during Henry VIII. Shakespeare retired to Stratford-upon-Avon and died in 1616.
Kind: event | Entities: william-shakespeare

**41** · **Isaac Newton Publishes the Principia and Rewrites the Laws of the Universe** (1687)
*A reclusive Cambridge professor explains gravity, motion, and the orbits of planets in one book*
On July 5, 1687, Isaac Newton's Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica was published in London, funded by astronomer Edmond Halley after the Royal Society ran out of money. In three volumes, Newton laid out the laws of motion and universal gravitation, explaining everything from falling apples to planetary orbits with a single mathematical framework. The Principia is widely regarded as the most important scientific work ever published. Newton was 44 and had developed the ideas twenty years earlier but never bothered to publish them until Halley coaxed him.
Kind: event | Entities: isaac-newton

**40** · **The Rosetta Stone Arrives at the British Museum After Britain Defeats Napoleon in Egypt** (1802)
*A slab of granodiorite becomes the key to deciphering 3,000 years of Egyptian hieroglyphics*
In 1802, the Rosetta Stone arrived at the British Museum as spoils of war after the French surrender in Egypt. The stone, discovered by Napoleon's soldiers in 1799 near the town of Rashid (Rosetta), bore the same decree in three scripts: hieroglyphic, Demotic, and Greek. It took another twenty years before Jean-François Champollion used the stone to crack the hieroglyphic code in 1822, unlocking 3,000 years of Egyptian history. The Rosetta Stone remains the British Museum's most visited object and Egypt's most requested repatriation.
Kind: event | Entities: napoleon-bonaparte

**39** · **Six Million Visitors Pour into the Crystal Palace for the Great Exhibition** (1851)
*A glass cathedral in Hyde Park showcases the peak of industrial civilization*
On May 1, 1851, Queen Victoria opened the Great Exhibition in Joseph Paxton's Crystal Palace — a revolutionary iron-and-glass structure covering 19 acres of Hyde Park. Over 140 days, six million people (a third of Britain's population) visited 100,000 exhibits from around the world, from the Koh-i-Noor diamond to early voting machines. The exhibition turned a profit of £186,000, which Prince Albert used to found the Victoria and Albert Museum, the Science Museum, and the Natural History Museum. The Crystal Palace was later moved to Sydenham Hill, where it burned down in 1936.
Kind: event | Entities: queen-victoria

**41** · **The Beatles Play Their Final Concert on a London Rooftop** (1969)
*Four musicians climb to a rooftop on Savile Row and play together for the last time*
On January 30, 1969, the Beatles climbed to the roof of their Apple Corps headquarters at 3 Savile Row and played an unannounced 42-minute concert that stopped traffic across central London. It was the band's first live performance in over two years and their last ever. Office workers watched from neighboring rooftops, crowds gathered in the street below, and the Metropolitan Police eventually arrived to shut it down. John Lennon's closing words — "I'd like to say thank you on behalf of the group and ourselves and I hope we passed the audition" — became one of rock's most famous sign-offs.
Kind: event | Entities: the-beatles

### The Holy Sites of Jerusalem
**ID**: `jerusalem-holy-sites` | **Type**: place | **Years**: 19 BCE–present | **Moments**: 5

> The square kilometer of ancient stone where three world religions converge — the holiest sites of Christianity, the last wall of the Jewish Temple, and the rock from which Muhammad ascended to heaven, all within walking distance of each other.

**83** · **Constantine Builds a Church Over the Site of the Crucifixion and Resurrection** (335)
*Where Christianity's holiest site has stood since 335 CE, enclosing both Golgotha and the empty tomb*
The Church of the Holy Sepulchre encompasses both Golgotha (Calvary), where Jesus was crucified, and the tomb where he was buried and resurrected — the holiest site in Christianity. Commissioned by Emperor Constantine in 326 CE after his mother Helena identified the locations, the church was consecrated in 335 CE. Destroyed by Caliph al-Hakim in 1009, rebuilt by the Crusaders, and shared today by six Christian denominations under an uneasy arrangement codified in 1853.
 | Entities: jesus

**37** · **Herod the Great Builds the Retaining Wall That Becomes Judaism's Holiest Prayer Site** (-19)
*Where the last surviving wall of the Second Temple has drawn Jewish prayers for two millennia*
The Western Wall is the last remaining retaining wall of the Second Temple complex, built by Herod the Great around 19 BCE as part of a massive expansion of the Temple Mount platform. After the Roman destruction of the Temple in 70 CE, the wall became the closest accessible point to the Holy of Holies and the holiest site where Jews are permitted to pray. The lower seven courses of massive stone blocks are original Herodian masonry. Millions of handwritten prayers are placed in the cracks between the stones each year.
 | Entities: king-david

**38** · **Caliph Abd al-Malik Builds the Dome of the Rock Over the Foundation Stone** (691)
*Where Muhammad ascended to heaven and Abraham prepared to sacrifice Isaac — sacred to two faiths on one rock*
Built between 685 and 691 CE by Umayyad Caliph Abd al-Malik, the Dome of the Rock is the oldest extant Islamic monument. In Islam, the rock beneath the golden dome is where Prophet Muhammad ascended to heaven during the Night Journey. In Judaism, this is the Foundation Stone where Abraham prepared to sacrifice Isaac and where the Holy of Holies of Solomon's Temple once stood. The octagonal structure with its gilded dome has defined the Jerusalem skyline for over 1,300 years.
 | Entities: abraham

**41** · **Jesus Prays in Agony and Is Betrayed by Judas in the Garden of Gethsemane** (33)
*Where the olive trees may be descendants of those under which Jesus spent his last free hours*
On the night before his crucifixion, Jesus withdrew to the Garden of Gethsemane at the foot of the Mount of Olives to pray. According to the Gospels, he asked God to "let this cup pass from me," sweating drops of blood. It was here that Judas Iscariot identified him with a kiss, leading to his arrest. The garden today contains olive trees estimated at 800–900 years old. The adjacent Church of All Nations, completed in 1924, was built over the remains of a 4th-century Byzantine church.
 | Entities: jesus

**41** · **Christians Establish the Via Dolorosa as the Path Jesus Carried His Cross** (33)
*Where 14 Stations of the Cross mark the 500-meter route through the Old City*
The Via Dolorosa is the processional route believed to trace the path Jesus walked carrying his cross from his condemnation by Pontius Pilate to his crucifixion at Golgotha. The 500-meter route passes through the Muslim and Christian Quarters of the Old City, marked by 14 Stations of the Cross — nine along the street and the final five inside the Church of the Holy Sepulchre. The current route was established in the 18th century. Franciscan friars have led processions along it every Friday afternoon since the 14th century.
 | Entities: jesus

### Biblical Sites of the Holy Land
**ID**: `holy-land-biblical-sites` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1500 BCE–1st century CE | **Moments**: 7

> From the cave where Jesus was born to the mountain where Moses received the commandments to the fortress where Jewish rebels made their last stand — the physical geography of the Bible.

**83** · **Constantine Builds a Church Over the Cave Where Jesus Was Born** (339)
*Where the oldest site in continuous Christian worship marks the birthplace of Jesus*
The Church of the Nativity in Bethlehem was built over the cave traditionally believed to be the birthplace of Jesus Christ. Commissioned by Emperor Constantine after his mother Helena's visit in 325–326 CE and dedicated in 339 CE, it is the oldest site continuously used as a place of Christian worship. A silver star on the marble floor of the underground grotto marks the exact traditional spot of the birth. The church was rebuilt by Byzantine Emperor Justinian after destruction during the Samaritan revolts.
 | Entities: jesus, mary-mother-jesus

**41** · **Jesus Makes Capernaum His Base for Ministry on the Sea of Galilee** (28)
*Where the ruins of Peter's house and the synagogue where Jesus preached still stand*
The fishing village of Capernaum on the northern shore of the Sea of Galilee served as Jesus's home base during his Galilean ministry. Here he called his first disciples, healed the centurion's servant, and preached in the synagogue. Excavations revealed a 4th-century house church built directly over what tradition identifies as Peter's house, later enclosed by an octagonal Byzantine church. The remains of a 4th-century limestone synagogue, built atop a 1st-century basalt synagogue, are among the most visited ruins in Israel.
 | Entities: jesus

**38** · **Justinian Builds a Monastery at the Foot of the Mountain Where Moses Received the Law** (565)
*Where one of the world's oldest monasteries guards the Burning Bush and ancient manuscripts*
St. Catherine's Monastery sits at the foot of Mount Sinai, where Moses is believed to have received the Ten Commandments. Built by Byzantine Emperor Justinian I between 548 and 565 CE, it is one of the oldest continuously operating Christian monasteries in the world. The monastery contains the world's second-largest collection of early codices and manuscripts after the Vatican, including the Codex Sinaiticus. It also houses what is venerated as the Burning Bush itself. Sacred to Christianity, Islam, and Judaism.
 | Entities: moses

**41** · **John the Baptist Baptizes Jesus in the Jordan River** (29)
*Where the site of the most famous baptism in history was closed by minefields until 2011*
Qasr el-Yahud on the Jordan River is the traditional site where John the Baptist baptized Jesus, marking the beginning of his public ministry. The same location is identified as where the Israelites crossed the Jordan into the Promised Land and where the prophet Elijah ascended to heaven. The site was surrounded by minefields from the 1967 war and remained closed for decades; it was only reopened to visitors in 2011 after a major demining effort.
 | Entities: jesus

**37** · **The Angel Gabriel Appears to Mary in Nazareth** (1969)
*Where the largest church in the Middle East marks the site of the Annunciation*
The Basilica of the Annunciation in Nazareth is built over the grotto where, according to Christian tradition, the Angel Gabriel told the Virgin Mary that she would conceive the Son of God. The current basilica — the largest church in the Middle East — was completed in 1969, the fifth church built on this site. The earliest was a 4th-century synagogue-church, followed by a Crusader-era cathedral. The lower level preserves the venerated grotto and remains of earlier structures.
 | Entities: mary-mother-jesus

**32** · **A Bedouin Shepherd Discovers the Dead Sea Scrolls in a Cave** (1947)
*Where 900 ancient manuscripts including the oldest Hebrew Bible texts were hidden for 2,000 years*
Between 1947 and 1956, over 900 manuscripts dating from the 3rd century BCE to the 1st century CE were discovered in eleven caves near the settlement of Qumran on the northwestern shore of the Dead Sea. The scrolls include the oldest known copies of books of the Hebrew Bible, predating previous manuscripts by a thousand years. They were hidden by what is believed to have been an Essene community. The first scrolls were found by a Bedouin shepherd searching for a lost goat.

**32** · **960 Jewish Rebels Choose Death Over Surrender on a Desert Fortress** (73)
*Where Herod's cliff-top palace became the site of Judaism's most famous last stand*
In 73 or 74 CE, after a three-year siege, the Roman Tenth Legion finally breached the walls of Masada — a cliff-top fortress overlooking the Dead Sea originally built by Herod the Great. According to the historian Josephus, the 960 Jewish Zealots inside chose mass suicide rather than capture. The Romans had constructed a massive assault ramp still visible today. Masada became a symbol of Jewish resistance and determination. Israeli soldiers historically took their oath of service at the summit.

### Artists, Composers, and Cultural Icons
**ID**: `artists-composers-icons` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1791–1980 | **Moments**: 5

> Van Gogh cutting off his ear in Arles, Mozart dying with his Requiem unfinished, Bob Marley playing through tear gas at Zimbabwe's independence, Kurosawa premiering the film that changed cinema, and Pelé scoring his 1,000th goal — the creators and performers who transcended their art.

**80** · **Van Gogh Cuts Off His Ear After a Confrontation with Gauguin in Arles** (1888)
*The painter who sold one painting in his lifetime produces 200 works in 15 months — then mutilates himself*
On December 23, 1888, Vincent van Gogh severed most of his left ear with a razor in the Yellow House in Arles, following an argument with Paul Gauguin. He wrapped the ear and delivered it to a woman at a nearby brothel. During his 15 months in Arles before the incident, Van Gogh had produced roughly 200 paintings in a manic burst of creativity. He sold only one painting in his lifetime and shot himself two years later at age 37.
Kind: event | Entities: vincent-van-gogh

**40** · **Mozart Dies at 35 and Is Buried in an Unmarked Grave in Vienna** (1791)
*The greatest musical prodigy in history dies mid-composition, leaving his Requiem unfinished*
On December 5, 1791, Mozart died in his Vienna apartment at age 35. He had been working feverishly on his Requiem in D minor — commissioned anonymously by a mysterious messenger — when illness overtook him. Despite over 600 compositions including 41 symphonies and iconic operas, Mozart had been struggling financially. He was buried in a common grave at St. Marx Cemetery; the exact location was never marked. The Requiem's commissioner was later revealed to be a count who planned to pass the work off as his own.
Kind: event | Entities: wolfgang-mozart

**38** · **Bob Marley Performs at Zimbabwe's Independence as Tear Gas Fills the Stadium** (1980)
*The voice of Third World liberation plays as a new African nation is born*
On April 17, 1980, Bob Marley and the Wailers performed at Zimbabwe's official independence ceremony at Rufaro Stadium in Harare. During the concert, police fired tear gas at crowds outside trying to get in. Marley stopped playing, the VIP section emptied, but Marley returned to the stage and finished his set while gas hung in the air. He considered Zimbabwe the most important performance of his life. He paid for the band's travel himself. Less than a year later, he was dead from melanoma at age 36.
Kind: event | Entities: bob-marley

**37** · **Akira Kurosawa Premieres Seven Samurai — the Film That Changed Cinema Forever** (1954)
*A three-hour samurai epic from Japan rewrites the grammar of action filmmaking worldwide*
On April 26, 1954, Akira Kurosawa's Seven Samurai premiered in Tokyo. The film — about seven masterless samurai defending a village from bandits — was the most expensive Japanese film ever made, went over budget, and nearly bankrupted Toho Studios. It became the most influential action film in cinema history. George Lucas adapted its plot for Star Wars, John Sturges remade it as The Magnificent Seven, and Kurosawa's techniques became the standard language of Hollywood filmmaking.
Kind: event | Entities: akira-kurosawa

**38** · **Pelé Scores His 1,000th Career Goal at the Maracanã** (1969)
*The greatest footballer in history reaches a milestone no one else has achieved*
On November 19, 1969, Pelé scored his 1,000th career goal on a penalty kick against Vasco da Gama at the Maracanã Stadium in Rio de Janeiro. The game was stopped as fans flooded the pitch. Pelé, in tears, was carried on shoulders. He had scored his first professional goal at 15 and won his first World Cup at 17 — the youngest player ever to do so. He won three World Cups total (1958, 1962, 1970), a feat unmatched. FIFA named him the greatest footballer of the 20th century.
Kind: event | Entities: pele

### Rome's Renaissance Masters
**ID**: `rome-renaissance-masters` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1512–1821 | **Moments**: 4

> The Sistine Chapel ceiling, the painter who died at 37 and was mourned by a Pope, a genius who committed murder, and a poet who died overlooking the Spanish Steps — Rome as the canvas for art's greatest dramas.

**78** · **Michelangelo Unveils the Sistine Chapel Ceiling After Four Years on Scaffolding** (1512)
*A sculptor who insisted he wasn't a painter creates the most famous ceiling in history*
On November 1, 1512, Pope Julius II celebrated Mass beneath the newly completed Sistine Chapel ceiling. Michelangelo had worked for four years (1508-1512), painting 343 figures across 5,000 square feet while lying on his back on scaffolding, paint dripping into his eyes. He wrote a poem about the misery: "My beard toward heaven...my brush, above my face, makes it a splendid floor by dripping down." The result — including The Creation of Adam with its almost-touching fingers — became the most iconic artwork of the Renaissance and possibly of all human civilization.
Kind: event | Entities: michelangelo

**29** · **Raphael Dies at Thirty-Seven and All of Rome Mourns** (1520)
*The painter who rivaled Michelangelo collapses after a fever and is buried in the Pantheon*
On April 6, 1520, Raphael Sanzio died in Rome at the age of 37, reportedly from a fever that developed after excessive physical exertion (Vasari attributed it to romantic excess). His death plunged Rome into mourning. Pope Leo X wept. Raphael's body was laid in state with his final masterpiece, the Transfiguration, displayed at his head. He was buried in the Pantheon at his own request, beneath an inscription that reads: "Here lies Raphael, by whom Nature feared to be outdone while he lived, and when he died, feared she herself would die." He had been painting the Vatican Stanze, rivaling Michelangelo's Sistine ceiling just rooms away.
Kind: event

**34** · **Caravaggio Kills a Man in a Street Brawl and Flees Rome Forever** (1606)
*The most revolutionary painter in Europe becomes a fugitive after a tennis court murder*
On May 29, 1606, Michelangelo Merisi da Caravaggio killed Ranuccio Tomassoni in a sword fight that erupted during or after a tennis match near the Campo Marzio. The exact cause of the quarrel is debated — gambling debts, a woman, or a political grudge. Caravaggio was wounded and fled Rome with a death sentence on his head. He spent the remaining four years of his life on the run through Naples, Malta, and Sicily, producing some of his greatest works while evading bounty hunters. He died in 1610 at age 38 under mysterious circumstances while trying to secure a papal pardon.
Kind: event | Entities: caravaggio

**28** · **John Keats Dies at Twenty-Five in a Room Overlooking the Spanish Steps** (1821)
*England's greatest Romantic poet succumbs to tuberculosis in a room he asked to be stripped bare*
On February 23, 1821, John Keats died of tuberculosis in a small room at 26 Piazza di Spagna, overlooking the Spanish Steps in Rome. He was 25 years old. In his final months, he asked that the room be stripped of all furnishings to prevent contamination — Italian health laws required everything to be burned after a consumptive's death. His last words to his friend Joseph Severn were: "Don't be frightened — be firm, and thank God it has come." His gravestone, which he dictated himself, reads: "Here lies One whose Name was writ in Water." The room is now the Keats-Shelley Memorial House museum.
Kind: event

### Artists and Writers Who Became Immortal
**ID**: `artists-writers-immortal` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 1824–1937 | **Moments**: 6

> A deaf composer who had to be turned around to see his ovation, an author who reinvented Christmas, a painter who turned her pain into self-portraits, and Picasso responding to a bombing with the most famous anti-war painting in history.

**78** · **A Completely Deaf Beethoven Premieres His Ninth Symphony and Has to Be Turned Around to See the Applause** (1824)
*The greatest composer in history conducts his masterpiece without hearing a single note*
On May 7, 1824, Ludwig van Beethoven's Symphony No. 9 in D minor premiered at the Theater am Kärntnertor in Vienna. Beethoven, completely deaf by this point, stood on stage turning pages and gesturing tempo, though the actual conducting was done by Michael Umlauf. When the performance ended, Beethoven was still facing the orchestra and didn't know the audience had erupted. The contralto Caroline Unger walked over and turned him around to see the standing ovation. The audience waved handkerchiefs and hats because they knew he couldn't hear their applause. The Ninth's "Ode to Joy" later became the anthem of the European Union.
Kind: event | Entities: beethoven

**39** · **Frida Kahlo Paints Her Pain into Masterpieces from a Bed in the Blue House** (1929)
*Crippled by a bus accident at eighteen, she turns her suffering into the most personal art in history*
Frida Kahlo was born, lived, and died in La Casa Azul (the Blue House) in Coyoacán, Mexico City. After a devastating bus accident at 18 shattered her spine, pelvis, and right leg, she began painting self-portraits from her bed using a mirror mounted on the canopy. Her 143 paintings — 55 of them self-portraits — depicted her physical agony, turbulent marriage to Diego Rivera, miscarriages, and Mexican identity with unflinching honesty. Dismissed during her lifetime as merely "Diego's wife," she became one of the most recognized and commercially valuable artists of the 20th century decades after her death in 1954.
Kind: event | Entities: frida-kahlo

**40** · **Picasso Paints Guernica in Five Weeks After the Bombing of a Basque Town** (1937)
*The most powerful anti-war painting in history is created in a fury of black and white*
On April 26, 1937, German and Italian bombers destroyed the Basque town of Guernica in Spain during the Civil War, killing hundreds of civilians. Pablo Picasso, living in Paris, was already commissioned to create a painting for the Spanish Pavilion at the Paris World's Fair. Abandoning his original plans, he completed Guernica — a massive 11-by-25-foot painting in black, white, and gray — in just five weeks. It depicted screaming women, a dismembered soldier, a bull, and a horse in agony. The painting became the most famous anti-war artwork in history. When a German officer allegedly asked Picasso "Did you do this?", he reportedly replied: "No, you did."
Kind: event | Entities: pablo-picasso

**39** · **Ernest Hemingway Finishes A Farewell to Arms in a Sweltering Key West Room** (1929)
*A hard-drinking war veteran writes the novel that defines a generation of American literature*
In 1929, Ernest Hemingway completed A Farewell to Arms while living in a second-floor room of a house on Whitehead Street in Key West, Florida. The novel, drawn from his experiences as an ambulance driver in World War I, cemented his reputation as the voice of the Lost Generation. Hemingway would spend the next decade in Key West, writing some of his greatest works including To Have and Have Not and several short stories. The house, with its famous colony of six-toed cats (descendants of a cat given to Hemingway by a ship's captain), is now a museum. He won the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1954.
Kind: event | Entities: ernest-hemingway

**39** · **Samuel Clemens Takes the Name "Mark Twain" from a River Depth Call on the Mississippi** (1863)
*A small-town boy from Missouri becomes America's greatest storyteller*
Samuel Clemens grew up in Hannibal, Missouri, on the banks of the Mississippi River. The river shaped everything: he became a steamboat pilot in 1859, adopted his pen name "Mark Twain" (a river term meaning "two fathoms deep" — safe water) in 1863, and set his two masterpieces — The Adventures of Tom Sawyer (1876) and Adventures of Huckleberry Finn (1885) — along these banks. Huck Finn, which Ernest Hemingway called the source of "all modern American literature," used the Mississippi as a mirror for America's deepest moral failure: slavery. Twain's boyhood home in Hannibal is now a museum.
Kind: event | Entities: mark-twain

**39** · **Charles Dickens Writes A Christmas Carol in Six Weeks and Reinvents Christmas** (1843)
*A desperate author's novella about a miser single-handedly revives a dying holiday*
In October 1843, Charles Dickens — in debt and anxious about declining sales — began writing A Christmas Carol. He finished it in six weeks, walking 15 to 20 miles through London at night to work out the plot. Published on December 19, 1843, the first edition of 6,000 copies sold out by Christmas Eve. The story of Scrooge's redemption is credited with reviving Christmas as a major celebration in Britain and America — the holiday had been fading in importance. The phrases "Merry Christmas" and "Bah! Humbug!" entered the English language. Dickens earned less than he hoped (he insisted on expensive binding), but he had permanently altered Western culture.
Kind: event | Entities: charles-dickens

### The Great Catholic Pilgrimage Sites
**ID**: `catholic-pilgrimage-sites` | **Type**: era | **Years**: 326 CE–present | **Moments**: 4

> The basilica built over Peter's bones, the cathedral at the end of a thousand-mile walk, and the grottos where the Virgin Mary appeared to children — the destinations that draw millions of Catholic pilgrims each year.

**76** · **Renaissance Masters Build the World's Largest Church Over Peter's Tomb** (1626)
*Where Michelangelo's dome crowns the burial site of Christianity's first pope*
St. Peter's Basilica in Vatican City was built over the traditional burial site of Saint Peter, one of Jesus's twelve apostles and the first Pope. The current Renaissance basilica was constructed between 1506 and 1626, replacing the 4th-century Constantinian basilica built around 326 CE. Designed by Bramante, Michelangelo, Maderno, and Bernini, it can hold over 20,000 worshippers. Michelangelo designed the iconic dome at age 71. Excavations beneath the basilica in the 1940s confirmed the presence of a 1st-century necropolis.
 | Entities: paul-apostle, michelangelo

**22** · **The Discovery of the Apostle James's Tomb Launches a Thousand Years of Pilgrimage** (1211)
*Where a thousand-mile walk across Europe ends at a medieval cathedral in Galicia*
The Cathedral of Santiago de Compostela is the endpoint of the Camino de Santiago (Way of St. James), one of the most important Christian pilgrimages since the Middle Ages. The cathedral contains the reputed relics of the Apostle James the Greater, discovered around 830 CE. Construction of the current cathedral began in 1075 and was consecrated in 1211. At its medieval peak, the Camino drew pilgrims from across Europe on foot, creating a network of routes, hostels, and hospitals across Spain and France that still function today.

**36** · **A 14-Year-Old Girl Reports Seeing the Virgin Mary at a Grotto in the Pyrenees** (1858)
*Where Bernadette Soubirous's visions turned a small French town into a global pilgrimage site*
Between February and July 1858, 14-year-old Bernadette Soubirous reported 18 apparitions of the Virgin Mary at a grotto in Lourdes, in the foothills of the French Pyrenees. The apparition identified herself as "the Immaculate Conception" and instructed Bernadette to dig in the ground, revealing a spring whose water many believe has healing properties. The Basilica of the Immaculate Conception was built above the grotto and consecrated in 1876. Approximately 5–6 million pilgrims visit annually. The Catholic Church has recognized 70 miraculous healings at Lourdes.
 | Entities: mary-mother-jesus

**36** · **Three Shepherd Children Report Visions of the Virgin Mary in Portugal** (1917)
*Where 70,000 people witnessed the "Miracle of the Sun" on October 13, 1917*
Between May and October 1917, three shepherd children — Lucia Santos and her cousins Francisco and Jacinta Marto — reported six apparitions of the Virgin Mary near Fatima, Portugal. The apparitions included the "Three Secrets of Fatima," among the most discussed prophecies in Catholic tradition. On October 13, the final apparition coincided with what up to 70,000 witnesses described as the sun dancing and zigzagging across the sky. The Basilica of Our Lady of the Rosary was completed in 1953. Approximately 6–8 million pilgrims visit annually.
 | Entities: mary-mother-jesus

### Frida Kahlo
**ID**: `frida-kahlo-life` | **Type**: biography | **Years**: 1925–1954 | **Moments**: 3

> A bus impales an 18-year-old through the pelvis. She paints self-portraits from her sickbed using a mirror. Her 143 paintings make her the most recognized Mexican artist in history.

**39** · **Frida Kahlo Paints Her Pain into Masterpieces from a Bed in the Blue House** (1929)
*Crippled by a bus accident at eighteen, she turns her suffering into the most personal art in history*
Frida Kahlo was born, lived, and died in La Casa Azul (the Blue House) in Coyoacán, Mexico City. After a devastating bus accident at 18 shattered her spine, pelvis, and right leg, she began painting self-portraits from her bed using a mirror mounted on the canopy. Her 143 paintings — 55 of them self-portraits — depicted her physical agony, turbulent marriage to Diego Rivera, miscarriages, and Mexican identity with unflinching honesty. Dismissed during her lifetime as merely "Diego's wife," she became one of the most recognized and commercially valuable artists of the 20th century decades after her death in 1954.
Kind: event | Entities: frida-kahlo

**76** · **A Bus Crash Impales an 18-Year-Old Through the Pelvis and Creates an Artist** (1925)
*The collision that broke Frida Kahlo's body and started one of the most extraordinary artistic careers in history*
On September 17, 1925, eighteen-year-old Frida Kahlo was riding a wooden bus that collided with an electric streetcar at the intersection of Calzada de Tlalpan and Cuauhtémotzin in Mexico City. A metal handrail pierced her pelvis, fracturing it in three places. Her spine was broken in three places, her collarbone and two ribs were snapped, and her right leg was shattered in eleven fractures. A fellow passenger recalled that gold powder from an artisan's packet covered her bleeding body. She would endure over 30 surgeries in her lifetime. Bedridden for months, she began painting self-portraits using a mirror her mother mounted above her bed — the beginning of one of the most personal bodies of work in art history.
Kind: event | Entities: frida-kahlo

**38** · **Frida Kahlo Attends Her Own Exhibition in an Ambulance and Dies Eight Days Later** (1954)
*Her first solo show in Mexico becomes her farewell — she arrives in her four-poster bed*
In April 1953, photographer Lola Álvarez Bravo organized Frida Kahlo's first solo exhibition in Mexico at the Galería de Arte Contemporáneo. Frida was so ill that doctors forbade her from attending. She went anyway — arriving by ambulance, she was carried in on a stretcher and placed in a four-poster bed installed in the center of the gallery. Guests celebrated around her as she held court from the bed, drinking and singing. It was her last public triumph. Her right leg was amputated below the knee that same year. On July 13, 1954, Frida Kahlo died at the Casa Azul at age 47. Her diary's last entry read: "I hope the exit is joyful — and I hope never to return."
Kind: event | Entities: frida-kahlo

### The Holy Cities of Islam
**ID**: `islamic-holy-cities` | **Type**: place | **Years**: 622 CE–present | **Moments**: 2

> The two holiest sites in Islam — the Kaaba in Mecca, which every Muslim must visit at least once, and the Prophet's Mosque in Medina, where Muhammad himself laid the first bricks.

**70** · **Abraham and Ishmael Build the Kaaba — Islam's Holiest Site and Center of the Hajj** (630)
*Where every Muslim must journey at least once — the holiest site in Islam*
The Kaaba, a cube-shaped structure draped in black silk at the center of the Masjid al-Haram in Mecca, is the holiest site in Islam. Muslims worldwide face the Kaaba during their five daily prayers. Islamic tradition holds that it was originally built by the Prophet Ibrahim (Abraham) and his son Ismail. Every able-bodied Muslim who can afford it must perform the Hajj pilgrimage here at least once in their lifetime, one of the Five Pillars of Islam. The Hajj draws approximately 2–3 million pilgrims annually.

**35** · **Muhammad Builds Islam's First Mosque, Now His Burial Place** (622)
*Where the Prophet laid the first bricks in 622 CE and where he was buried a decade later*
Al-Masjid an-Nabawi (the Prophet's Mosque) in Medina is the second holiest site in Islam. Originally built by the Prophet Muhammad himself in 622 CE after his migration from Mecca, it was one of the first mosques in Islamic history. The mosque now contains the tombs of Muhammad and the first two caliphs, Abu Bakr and Umar, beneath the distinctive Green Dome added in 1279 CE and painted green in 1837. Expanded many times over 14 centuries, it is now one of the largest mosques in the world.

**34** · **Willie Nelson Unites Hippies and Cowboys at the Armadillo** (1972)

On August 12, 1972, Willie Nelson stepped onto the stage at the Armadillo World Headquarters and changed the trajectory of American music. Before this night, country music and rock and roll were on opposite sides of a cultural war. Willie, having fled the rigid hierarchies of Nashville, appeared with long hair and a beat-up guitar, bridging the gap between conservative cowboys and hippie counterculture. For the first time, rednecks and longhairs shared the same dance floor, giving birth to the "cosmic cowboy" movement.

Willie Taliaferro Nelson was born April 29, 1933, in this tiny cotton town. Raised by his grandparents, he took music lessons from mail-order catalogs and wrote his first song at age seven. The town of Abbott (population 356) remains a pilgrimage site for fans, where the small Methodist church he once attended is still standing.

Despite the mayhem, the Picnic became a foundational myth for the Austin music scene, an annual rite of passage that has continued for over 50 years.

### The O. Henry Embezzlement Mystery
**ID**: `o-henry-embezzlement` | **Type**: incident | **Years**: 1894–1898 | **Moments**: 6

> Before he was the world-famous short story writer O. Henry, William Sydney Porter was a bumbling bank teller who fled to Honduras after being accused of stealing $854.08. He turned his eventual jail time into a literary career.

**32** · **William Sydney Porter Coins the Name 'Servant Girl Annihilators'** (1885)
*An Austin bank clerk provides the moniker that defines the case*
Living just blocks from the scenes of the Servant Girl Annihilator murders in 1885, Austin bank clerk William Sydney Porter—later famous as O. Henry—coined the term 'Annihilator.' In a letter to his friend Dave Hall, he wrote: 'Town is fearfully dull... except for the frequent raids of the Servant Girl Annihilators who make things lively in the dull hours of the night.' This offhand remark became one of the first American serial killer nicknames.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry, servant-girl-annihilator

**64** · **William Sydney Porter Is Accused of Embezzling from First National Bank** (1894)
*A bank shortage leads to the indictment of the future 'O. Henry'*
Porter worked as a teller at 6th and Congress. When federal bank examiners found a shortage in his accounts, he was accused of embezzlement. Porter panicked and fled to New Orleans, then Honduras, before the illness of his wife forced him to return to Austin to face trial.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **William Sydney Porter Is Detained While Awaiting Sentencing** (1898)
*A future literary legend begins writing stories from his cell*
Porter was held here after his arrest. While awaiting sentencing, he began writing short stories to support his daughter. He adopted the pen name 'O. Henry' to hide his criminal past from editors, a name that would soon become more famous than his own.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

When William Sydney Porter arrived in Austin in 1884, he worked as a pharmacist at the Morley Brothers Drug Store on East 8th Street. It was here that he began sketching local characters and eavesdropping on conversations that would later fuel his short stories. At the time, Austin was a growing frontier town of 11,000 people, providing the young Porter with a wealth of narrative inspiration.

In 1887, Porter took a job as a draftsman at the Texas General Land Office, now the oldest state office building in Texas. He spent four years here mapping the wild geography of the state. The thick stone walls and bureaucratic atmosphere left a deep impression, later serving as the setting for his story "Bexar Scrip No. 2692." This was a period of stability where he married Athol Estes and began his humor magazine, The Rolling Stone.

Porter was a fixture at Scholz Garden, Austin's oldest biergarten and political hub. He was a gifted singer and a member of the Hill City Quartette, which performed German lieder and folk songs for the regulars. Scholz was more than a bar; it was where the town's German heritage and Texas politics collided. The bronze statue at Auditorium Shores commemorates the legacy of Stevie Ray Vaughan. Following his death in a 1990 helicopter crash, the site became a central point for music pilgrimages. It stands near the location of many of his most famous performances, where fans from around the world gather to pay their respects to the guitar legend.

### O. Henry — Life & Legacy
**ID**: `o-henry-life` | **Type**: biography | **Years**: 1862–1910 | **Moments**: 17

> Follow the picaresque life of William Sydney Porter, who transformed from a bumbling Austin bank clerk into the world-famous short story master O. Henry. From elopements and embezzlement to a fugitive exile in Honduras and a prison cell in Ohio, his life was a sequence of ironic twists that mirrored his own legendary fiction.

**32** · **William Sydney Porter Coins the Name 'Servant Girl Annihilators'** (1885)
*An Austin bank clerk provides the moniker that defines the case*
Living just blocks from the scenes of the Servant Girl Annihilator murders in 1885, Austin bank clerk William Sydney Porter—later famous as O. Henry—coined the term 'Annihilator.' In a letter to his friend Dave Hall, he wrote: 'Town is fearfully dull... except for the frequent raids of the Servant Girl Annihilators who make things lively in the dull hours of the night.' This offhand remark became one of the first American serial killer nicknames.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry, servant-girl-annihilator

**64** · **William Sydney Porter Is Accused of Embezzling from First National Bank** (1894)
*A bank shortage leads to the indictment of the future 'O. Henry'*
Porter worked as a teller at 6th and Congress. When federal bank examiners found a shortage in his accounts, he was accused of embezzlement. Porter panicked and fled to New Orleans, then Honduras, before the illness of his wife forced him to return to Austin to face trial.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **William Sydney Porter Works as a Pharmacist at Morley Brothers** (1884)
*A young clerk observes the frontier characters who will fill his stories*
When William Sydney Porter arrived in Austin in 1884, he worked as a pharmacist at the Morley Brothers Drug Store on East 8th Street. It was here that he began sketching local characters and eavesdropping on conversations that would later fuel his short stories. At the time, Austin was a growing frontier town of 11,000 people, providing the young Porter with a wealth of narrative inspiration.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **William Sydney Porter Works as a Draftsman at the General Land Office** (1887)
*Porter spends four years mapping Texas and finding literary inspiration*
In 1887, Porter took a job as a draftsman at the Texas General Land Office, now the oldest state office building in Texas. He spent four years here mapping the wild geography of the state. The thick stone walls and bureaucratic atmosphere left a deep impression, later serving as the setting for his story "Bexar Scrip No. 2692." This was a period of stability where he married Athol Estes and began his humor magazine, The Rolling Stone.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **William Sydney Porter Sings with the Hill City Quartette at Scholz Garden** (1886)
*Bohemian life and German beer at Austin's oldest continuously operated bar*
Porter was a fixture at Scholz Garden, Austin's oldest biergarten and political hub. He was a gifted singer and a member of the Hill City Quartette, which performed German lieder and folk songs for the regulars. Scholz was more than a bar; it was where the town's German heritage and Texas politics collided. The museum still displays the chair and table he used, a physical link to the bohemian lifestyle he enjoyed before the embezzlement charges forced him into exile and prison.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry, scholz-garden

**64** · **Orphaned Boy Apprentices as a Pharmacist in Greensboro** (1862)
*Raised in a drugstore in Greensboro*
Porter was born September 11, 1862, in Greensboro, NC. His mother died when he was only three, and he was raised by his aunt in a house filled with books and sketching paper. At age 15, he began an apprenticeship at his uncle's drugstore, eventually becoming a licensed pharmacist. The regulars at the shop became the prototypes for his later characters.
 | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **Porter Flees Embezzlement Charges to Honduras** (1896)
*Fleeing the law in a country with no treaty*
When federal auditors indicted Porter for a $5,654.20 shortage at the First National Bank in 1896, he made a snap decision that ruined his reputation: he fled. He took a train to New Orleans and then a steamer to Honduras, one of the few countries without a US extradition treaty. He spent six months living as a bohemian, reportedly befriending the infamous bank robber Al Jennings.
 | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **Porter Adopts the Pen Name O. Henry in Prison** (1898)
*A master writer is born in a prison cell*
Convicted in February 1898, Porter served three years in the Ohio State Penitentiary in Columbus. His training as a pharmacist saved him from hard labor; he was assigned to the prison hospital. In the quiet of the night shifts, he began writing short stories to support his daughter, adopting the pen name "O. Henry" to protect his identity. He published 14 stories from prison.
 | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **O. Henry Dies in a New York Hotel Room at Forty-Seven** (1910)
*The master of the twist ending dies in the dark*
After prison, Porter moved to New York City in 1902 and entered a period of staggering productivity, writing a story a week for the New York World. He became a celebrity, known for his reclusive habits. He died at age 47 in a New York hotel room from cirrhosis. His last words were a final touch of O. Henry irony: "Turn up the lights. I don't want to go home in the dark."
 | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **Elopes with Athol Estes at Smoot Terrace** (1887)
*A midnight marriage despite a mother's fierce opposition*
William Sydney Porter eloped with 17-year-old Athol Estes on July 1, 1887, after her mother forbade the union due to Athol's delicate health. They fled to the home of Reverend R. K. Smoot, where they were married in the parlor. This secret ceremony marked the beginning of Porter's most stable and creatively formative years in Austin.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **Launches 'The Rolling Stone' Humor Weekly** (1894)
*A short-lived satirical paper that mocked Austin's elite*
Porter rented a small office at 113 East 7th Street to launch his own satirical weekly, The Rolling Stone. He wrote, illustrated, and typeset the paper, which featured local gossip, political cartoons, and early drafts of his short stories. While the paper eventually failed, it provided the laboratory where he perfected the "O. Henry" style of dry, observational wit.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **Faces Judgment at the Federal Courthouse** (1898)
*A career-ending conviction for national bank embezzlement*
In February 1898, Porter stood trial at the old U.S. Post Office and Courthouse for embezzling funds from the First National Bank. Despite a lack of clear evidence and his defense of "sloppy bookkeeping," the jury found him guilty. The verdict effectively ended his life in Texas and set him on the path to the Ohio Penitentiary, where he would reinvent himself as a writer.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **Observes the "Four Million" from Irving Place** (1903)
*The brownstone where Porter became the voice of New York*
Porter lived in this modest room near Gramercy Park during his peak creative years from 1903 to 1906. He was known to sit by the window or wander the nearby streets, observing the shopgirls, clerks, and vagrants he called "The Four Million." It was here he wrote most of the stories that defined the New York experience for a generation of readers.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **Writes 'The Furnished Room' in a Chelsea Boarding House** (1904)
*Transforming urban loneliness into a literary masterpiece*
In a somber Chelsea rooming house, Porter penned one of his most haunting stories, "The Furnished Room." The narrative, about a young man searching for his lost love in the transient shadows of the city, reflected Porter's own sense of isolation and his deep empathy for New York's "drifters." This location remains a symbol of the melancholic side of his urban realism.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**32** · **Drafts 'The Gift of the Magi' at Pete's Tavern** (1905)
*A holiday classic born in the second booth from the door*
Porter was a regular at this historic tavern, where he often wrote to meet his weekly deadlines. Legend holds that he drafted "The Gift of the Magi" in a single sitting in the second booth from the entrance. The tavern's atmosphere, unchanged for a century, still honors the writer who found beauty in the simple sacrifices of the working class.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **Marries Childhood Sweetheart Sara Coleman** (1907)
*A brief attempt at domestic stability in the Blue Ridge*
Hoping to escape his mounting debts and alcoholism in New York, Porter married his childhood sweetheart, Sara Lindsay Coleman, in Asheville. They moved into a house in the Blue Ridge Mountains, but the marriage was strained by Porter's inability to leave his urban habits behind. He eventually returned to New York alone, leaving his final attempt at a traditional life behind.
Kind: event | Entities: o-henry

**31** · **Seeks Refuge at the Hotel Chelsea** (1907)
*A final, desperate retreat for a fading literary giant*
In his final years, plagued by ill health and a loss of creative spark, Porter stayed at the iconic Hotel Chelsea. He lived a reclusive existence, often avoiding his editors and friends as his physical condition deteriorated. The hotel, already a magnet for creative misfits, served as one of his last temporary shelters before his final admission to the hospital.
Kind: presence | Entities: o-henry

### Diego Rivera
**ID**: `diego-rivera-murals` | **Type**: biography | **Years**: 1929–1934 | **Moments**: 3

> A 300-pound communist paints the history of Mexico across the national palace, then crosses the border and paints capitalism at its source — when he includes Lenin, Rockefeller destroys the mural.

**64** · **Diego Rivera Paints the Entire History of Mexico Across the Walls of the National Palace** (1929)
*A 300-pound communist transforms the seat of Mexican power into the largest mural cycle in the world*
In 1929, Diego Rivera began painting a massive cycle of murals across the main stairwell and second-floor corridors of the Palacio Nacional in Mexico City. Working over the next 22 years (1929–1951), he covered over 1,500 square meters of wall space with scenes spanning from the Aztec world of Tenochtitlan through the Spanish conquest, colonial rule, independence, and the Mexican Revolution. The central panel depicts the eagle on a cactus — the founding myth of Tenochtitlan — surrounded by scenes of exploitation, resistance, and revolution. Rivera used a technique called true fresco, painting directly onto wet plaster, making the images literally part of the building. The murals remain open to the public for free inside the working seat of Mexico's executive branch.
Kind: event | Entities: diego-rivera

**32** · **Rivera Paints Ford's Assembly Line and Detroit Nearly Destroys the Murals** (1932)
*The world's most famous communist paints capitalism at its most powerful — and the city nearly whitewashes it*
In 1932, Edsel Ford commissioned Diego Rivera to paint murals for the garden court of the Detroit Institute of Arts. Rivera spent months studying the Ford River Rouge plant — the world's largest factory complex — and produced 27 panels depicting the full cycle of automobile production. He included images of workers, machines, raw materials, and scientific research, framing industrial labor as the equal of any subject in classical art. When unveiled in March 1933, religious leaders and editorialists called the murals pornographic and blasphemous, demanding they be whitewashed. Edsel Ford refused, and over 86,000 people visited in the first week. The Detroit Industry Murals are now considered the finest work of public art in America and are a National Historic Landmark.
Kind: event | Entities: diego-rivera

**32** · **Rockefeller Pays Rivera to Paint a Mural, Then Destroys It Because It Contains Lenin** (1934)
*The most notorious act of art censorship in American history happens at 30 Rockefeller Plaza*
In 1933, Nelson Rockefeller commissioned Diego Rivera to paint a 63-foot mural titled "Man at the Crossroads" in the lobby of 30 Rockefeller Plaza in New York City. When Rivera included a portrait of Vladimir Lenin in the composition, Rockefeller asked him to replace it with an anonymous face. Rivera refused. On May 9, 1933, Rivera was paid his full fee of $21,000 and escorted from the building by security. In February 1934, workers smashed the mural to pieces with axes in the middle of the night. Rivera's response was to repaint the work — even larger — in the Palacio de Bellas Artes in Mexico City, this time adding a portrait of John D. Rockefeller Sr. drinking with syphilis bacteria floating nearby. That version survives today.
Kind: event | Entities: diego-rivera

### Day of the Dead
**ID**: `day-of-the-dead` | **Type**: place | **Years**: 1500 BC–present | **Moments**: 3

> Three thousand years before it became a UNESCO tradition, the Aztecs celebrated a festival of the dead presided over by the goddess Mictecacíhuatl. The Spanish tried to erase it. Instead, it absorbed Catholicism and became the most visually striking ritual in the Americas — families spending the night in cemeteries surrounded by marigold petals and candlelight.

**64** · **The Aztecs Dedicate an Entire Month to the Dead — Presided Over by the Lady of the Underworld** (-1500)
*Mictecacíhuatl, the goddess who swallowed the stars, ruled a festival the Spanish could not kill*
Long before the Spanish arrived, the Aztecs dedicated the ninth month of their calendar — roughly August — to a festival of the dead presided over by Mictecacíhuatl, the Lady of the Dead who ruled the underworld alongside her husband Mictlantecuhtli. Skulls were displayed as trophies, and offerings of food and marigolds were placed for the departed. When the Spanish attempted to replace the tradition with Catholic All Saints' Day in the 16th century, indigenous communities absorbed the new dates (November 1–2) but kept their rituals. The result was a syncretic tradition that survives across Mexico today.
Kind: milestone

**33** · **Families Spend the Entire Night in a Cemetery on an Island Lit by Ten Thousand Candles** (1500)
*On Janitzio Island in Lake Pátzcuaro, the Purépecha keep the most ancient Day of the Dead vigil in Mexico*
On the night of November 1, families on Janitzio Island in Lake Pátzcuaro carry ofrendas — altars laden with marigolds, sugar skulls, pan de muerto, and the favorite foods of the deceased — to the island's small hilltop cemetery. They spend the entire night beside the graves, lighting thousands of candles that turn the cemetery into a glowing beacon visible across the lake. The Purépecha people of Janitzio have maintained this vigil for centuries, making it the oldest continuously practiced Day of the Dead tradition in Mexico. UNESCO inscribed the holiday as Intangible Cultural Heritage in 2008.
Kind: presence

**32** · **A Mexico City Village Illuminates Its Cemetery with Thousands of Candles in the Alumbrada** (1600)
*San Andrés Mixquic draws hundreds of thousands to witness graves blanketed in marigold petals and candlelight*
On the evening of November 2, the village of San Andrés Mixquic in southeast Mexico City performs the Alumbrada — a ceremony in which every grave in the village cemetery is covered in cempasúchil (marigold) petals and lit by dozens of candles. The marigolds form a carpet of orange from the cemetery entrance to each family's graves, creating a path the dead are said to follow home. Mixquic, whose name means "place of the dead" in Nahuatl, was a pre-Columbian settlement built on a lake island. Today it draws hundreds of thousands of visitors on a single night.
Kind: presence
